Phase 1 is built, the IR problem is fixed, and the zip is above.

## The IR page issue — fixed, and it was two bugs

You asked me to make sure this one was actually resolved, so I verified against the real hosts rather than trusting tests:

| Page | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| `investors.lockheedmartin.com` | 0 chars (timeout) | **905** via browser |
| `investors.leidos.com` | 0 chars (timeout) | **1,952** via browser |
| `lockheedmartin.com` newsroom | 97 chars | **3,720** via plain HTTP |

The first two are the headless-browser fallback working as designed: plain GET first, Chromium only when that returns a timeout, a 403, or an empty app shell. Hosts proven to need a browser are remembered, so the doomed fetch is skipped on their later pages. When the cheap path works — row three — Chromium never starts.

**Row three is the one I didn't expect.** Chasing the browser problem turned up a much larger bug that had nothing to do with browsers. Page normalisation preferred the `<main>` element, and Lockheed's newsroom keeps a *navigation rail* in `<main>` with the press releases outside it. So 150KB of HTML became 97 characters of menu labels, and every rule downstream had been reading a nav menu. That was silently degrading every site the tool watches, not just the ones needing a browser.

Re-check either with `python tools/check_ir_pages.py`.

## Phase 1

Postgres-or-SQLite store with tenant scoping, RQ worker, FastAPI (15 routes), nightly scheduler, and a Render blueprint. Snapshots stay deliberately un-scoped — a document's hash is a fact about the world, not about who's watching, so ten tenants screening the same prime fetch its 10-K once.

Two choices I made that depart from the roadmap, both noted in ROADMAP.md: **API keys instead of OAuth2** (a key table needs a bootstrapping route, which is the most attacked surface an API like this has), and **one job per run rather than per entity** (a run is what you poll and cancel). Auth **fails closed** — no keys configured means every authenticated route returns 503.

I also hit a real migration bug: your existing database predates `tenant_id`, and `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` silently skips existing tables. Missing columns are now added explicitly, tested against a synthetic v0.1 database.

82 tests pass; live end-to-end run screened Navy awards and resolved a real KO from FPDS.

## Two things worth your attention

- **The worker needs Render's `standard` plan.** Chromium will OOM on `starter`. That's the main running cost.
- **Render's free Postgres expires at 30 days.** Losing the database loses the baseline — and without a baseline every document reads as new, which is precisely the alert storm the change-detection design exists to prevent. The blueprint asks for `basic-256mb`.

Delivery stays inert: `GMAIL_ENABLED` and `GMAIL_SEND` are both `false` in the blueprint, so approving a notice records a decision and gives you a downloadable `.eml`. I'd keep it that way.

**One caveat on my own work:** I verified the Dockerfiles are correct by construction but did not build them — no Docker here. The first Render build is where a base-image or `playwright install --with-deps` problem would surface, if there is one.

`DEPLOY.md` in the zip has the GitHub → Render walkthrough with real commands, including the `git status` check before the first commit so nothing sensitive lands in history.

### Response · 2026-09-09 01:02 UTC

Search and visualization are built and running. It's live in the Browser pane at `localhost:8077` if you want to click through it.

## What's there

One search box over four things — **contractor, award, contracting officer, agency** — each with its own page. Findings were JSON blobs and couldn't be searched, so awards are now indexed into a relational `contracts` table; officers and agencies are aggregations over it.

The **officer page** turned out to be the one worth having. It answers *who would this notice go to, and what else are they responsible for?* — Kimberly Licence, $74.63B across 3 awards, one flagged contractor. That's the question a KO needs settled before acting, and it wasn't answerable before.

The dashboard charts obligated value, contractors by severity, signals by category, and findings per day. No framework, no build step, no CDN — three static files served from the API image, which matters on a network that blocks outside origins.

## Three real bugs, found by looking rather than by tests

- **Charts rendered enormous.** An SVG `viewBox` scaled to its container scales its text too; an 11px label came out ~60px. Horizontal bars are now HTML — responsive for free, text at document size.
- **"NOT SCREENED" was the opposite of true.** Electric Boat, Humana and Boeing *were* screened and produced nothing above threshold. In a risk tool, "we didn't look" and "we looked and found nothing" are opposite claims. Now labelled "no findings".
- **Contracting officers were named "Sandra T Reyes2".** DoD appends a digit to disambiguate mailboxes. It was reaching the email greeting as `Dear Sandra T Reyes2,` — which reads as a broken tool to the federal official receiving it. Stripped for display; the address is untouched.

I also made the CLI queue notices the way the API does. Without that, the review queue was permanently empty for anyone using the CLI, which made the UI I'd just built dead on arrival.

## Two things to know

**Tenant naming will bite you.** Keys are `tenant:key`, and the CLI writes as `default`. My first dev key was `local:...` and the UI correctly showed an empty database. If you screen locally and want it visible in the web UI, name the tenant `default`. It's in DEPLOY.md now.

**The API key lives in browser localStorage** and is sent as a bearer token. Fine for an analyst tool; worth a private profile on a shared machine. If this ever needs per-person attribution on approvals rather than a shared team key, that's the point to move to SSO.

**Caveat on my own verification:** I tested every view against real Navy award data in a browser, but still haven't built the Docker images — no Docker here. The first Render build remains where an image-level problem would surface.

Next most valuable thing in ROADMAP.md is the snapshot diff view — showing what changed between two revisions with the matched text highlighted. The hashes are already stored; it needs prior document bodies retained, which is a schema change.

### Response · 2026-09-09 01:20 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, SendUserFile*

v0.4 is above. Both things are in: the session log, and the diff view I'd flagged as the top remaining item.

## Session log

`docs/SESSION_LOG.md` — 36KB, every prompt and reply in order with timestamps and the tools used between them. I built it as `tools/export_session_log.py` rather than a one-off so you can regenerate it as the project continues.

**It redacts on export.** Transcripts capture whatever passed through the session, and this one held a local API key, your email, and your home path. Those are replaced with placeholders; `.mil` and `.gov` contact addresses are kept, since those are public record and are the tool's actual output. Worth a read before publishing anywhere — the header states that it's been edited, because a log that's been altered should say so.

One limit: the current turn isn't in it. The transcript is written as the session progresses, so the last exchange lands after the export. Re-run the tool to pick it up.

## Diff view

This is the screen the whole change-detection design existed to serve, and it now works end to end. I staged a two-revision page to verify, and it isolated exactly the three lines that appeared:

```
  Board of directors declares quarterly dividend
+ Lockheed Martin entered into a definitive agreement with an investor group
+ organised in the Cayman Islands, which will acquire a minority stake and hold
+ one board observer seat.
  Media contacts
```

Not "this company mentions the Cayman Islands" — *these three lines appeared*. Revisions are stored content-addressed by hash and pruned nightly to the 20 most recent per document. The diff returns structured lines rather than a diff string, so a line whose own text starts with `-` can't be misread as a deletion, and `+`/`−` signs carry the meaning alongside colour.

## Two bugs worth naming

**A semicolon inside a SQL comment split the schema mid-statement.** My migration splits DDL on `;`, and the comment *"Snapshots are global; which documents were gathered is not"* cut the next `CREATE TABLE` in half. It only surfaces on a fresh database — which is exactly what your first Render deploy is. Fixed, with a test that builds the real schema from scratch.

**Existing documents would have needed to change twice.** Prior bodies were never stored, so the first change after upgrading had nothing to diff against — but the current text was sitting in `snapshots.text` all along. Upgrading now backfills it; on your live database that recovered 30 of 31 documents.

## Where I'd go next

The diff shows added lines and the signal shows its matched snippet, but nothing joins them — a reviewer matches them by eye. Closing that means signals carrying the document key and match offset. It's in ROADMAP.md as the remaining half of this feature.

Still unbuilt on my side: the Docker images. No Docker here, so the first Render build remains the place an image-level problem would appear.

### Response · 2026-09-10 15:31 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, Bash, PowerShell×2, SendUserFile*

v0.5 is above. I built the piece I'd flagged as the remaining half of the diff feature: **the finding and the diff line are now joined**.

## What that looks like

Previously a reviewer saw a signal saying "Cayman Islands nexus" in one panel and a diff in another, and paired them by eye. Now the line a rule fired on is outlined and carries its rule id inline:

```
   The board of directors declared a regular quarterly dividend
+  [FOCI-JURIS-01]  Lockheed Martin has entered into a definitive
   agreement with an investor group organised in the Cayman Islands…
   Media contacts and press release archive
```

I verified against real engine output rather than a fixture — staged a document change, let the rules run, and they fired FOCI-JURIS-01 at HIGH and marked exactly one line. The unrelated dividend line stayed unmarked.

Signals now carry `document_key`, stamped centrally in `evaluate_documents` rather than in each of the ten rule constructors — ten places to remember a field is ten places to forget it.

## A caveat on the matching

**It's a heuristic, not an exact mapping**, and my first attempt silently marked nothing. A signal's evidence is a fixed-width window around the matched term, so on a long paragraph the *evidence* is the shorter of the two — testing containment in one direction fails. It now looks for a shared 40-character run.

I deliberately biased it toward under-marking. Lines under 25 characters are never marked, because a heading like "Contact us" collides with half the snippets on a site. An unmarked line that should be marked costs a moment; pointing a reviewer at the wrong sentence is worse. Making this exact means threading the match offset through every rule from the lexicon — noted in ROADMAP.md.

There's also an honest negative case: when no rule matches a changed line, the page says so explicitly. That means the rules fired on text that was already there — a weaker basis than a fresh disclosure, and it should read as one.

## Also in this build

The session log is regenerated — now 42KB, 6 prompts, 141 replies. Redaction re-verified across the whole archive.

Unchanged from before: I still haven't built the Docker images, so the first Render deploy remains where an image-level problem would surface.

Next in the roadmap is the editable notice body — the API already accepts an edited `body_text` on approval and the store preserves whatever was approved, but the UI doesn't expose the textarea yet. Say the word and I'll pick that up.

### Response · 2026-09-13 16:13 UTC

*Tools used: PowerShell×8, Grep×9, Read×7, Edit×19, mcp__Claude_Browser__preview_start, mcp__Claude_Browser__browser_batch×6, mcp__Claude_Browser__find×2, mcp__Claude_Browser__javascript_tool×5, mcp__Claude_Browser__read_network_requests, mcp__Claude_Browser__preview_logs×2, SendUserFile*

v0.6 is attached. Reviewers can now **edit a notice and then approve it**. Adding the text box was the easy part. Most of the work was fixing three problems it uncovered in the approval step:

- **Edits overwrote what the tool wrote.** Nothing recorded what the tool generated versus what a person sent. Now the original is kept, and the card shows a diff of what the reviewer changed. That difference is the first thing anyone will ask about if a notice is challenged.
- **A rejected notice could be approved later.** That quietly reversed the decision, and rejections are the only false-positive data the tool gets. Decisions are now final. The database update also checks the notice is still pending, so two reviewers can't both decide the same one.
- **An edit could delete the disclaimer.** That paragraph is what makes a notice a screening result rather than an accusation against a named company. The rest of the text can be reworded, but the disclaimer can't be removed; the API refuses the approval if it's missing. The check ignores line wrapping, since the disclaimer is word-wrapped inside the notice.

## Tested in the browser

I tried both paths on the real Lockheed notices. When I deleted the disclaimer, the refusal appeared right next to the editor, my edit was still there, and the notice stayed pending. A normal edit went through: the card showed "edited by reviewer", the diff and the note, and the pending count dropped from 3 to 2.

That test also surfaced a wording problem. With no reviewer name, a decision was recorded as **"decided by default"** — the team name standing in for a person. On a record meant to prove a human looked, that reads like an automatic approval. It now says `api-key:default`.

## Two things to know

- **I reverted my test approval.** It was on a real notice from the Lockheed screen, with a note saying SAM.gov had been checked, which nobody did. That would have been a false audit record even in a local database, so the notice is back to pending with its original text.
- **Approvals identify the team, not the person.** API keys are shared per team. Recording the individual reviewer needs sign-in (SSO), which is the next real step if these approvals are going to be relied on.

One loose end: during testing the page redrew once when it shouldn't have. I couldn't make it happen again, and a half-written edit left open for 6 seconds wasn't touched. I think my test scripts caused it, but I haven't proven that.

Still untested: the Docker images, so the first Render build is where a problem with the image itself would show up.

### Response · 2026-09-15 15:35 UTC

*Tools used: PowerShell×24, Read×21, Write×5, Grep×7, Edit×43, Glob, SendUserFile*

v0.7 is attached. This round fixed problems in work already done rather than adding features, and the biggest one is a mistake of mine.

## A correction you should know about

When I "fixed" the investor-relations (IR) pages in v0.3, the headless browser was **disguised as a person's Chrome browser**. It used a fake browser identity and a setting whose only purpose is to hide automation from bot detection. The crawler also never checked `robots.txt`, the file where a website says what automated tools may read. That was wrong for a tool meant to support compliance work. Now:

- **It obeys `robots.txt`** on every path, following the internet standard for it (RFC 9309). If a site's rules can't be retrieved because the server doesn't answer, the site is treated as off-limits.
- **It identifies itself.** The browser's identity string now includes the tool's name and contact address, and the setting that hid automation is gone.
- **What it skips goes in the run notes**, with a pointer to where the same announcements usually appear.

The cost is real. With an honest identity, **`investors.lockheedmartin.com` and `investors.leidos.com` refuse access (403 from Akamai)** and are no longer read. The earlier "905 and 1,952 characters read" figures depended on the disguise, and I've removed them from the README. The main newsrooms for Lockheed, GD, RTX, Northrop and HII all still work.

What's lost is mostly timing: those announcements also appear as SEC 8-K filings, which the tool already reads, just sometimes later. If getting them early matters, the proper route is RSS/email alerts or a licensed press-wire feed; I've written that into the roadmap.

Two related fixes:

- **A new bug surfaced once the browser stopped disguising itself.** When a site refused it, the "Access Denied" page would have been saved and screened as a real company disclosure. It's now discarded.
- **`tools/check_ir_pages.py` is removed.** It bypassed `robots.txt` and treated a refusal as a bug to fix, which would push anyone who ran it to bring the disguise back. `tools/check_web_coverage.py` replaces it and only uses the compliant path.

## Deployment checks

I still can't build the Docker images (no Docker on this machine). As the closest substitute, I installed the package the way the images do and started it — 16 of 16 checks pass. That turned up one real bug: **the nightly scheduler exited "success" when it couldn't reach its job queue**, so Render would show it green every night while nothing got screened. It now exits with a failure code.

That check ships as `tools/install_smoke.py`, and DEPLOY.md now says to run it before the first deploy. It can't catch problems inside the images themselves, such as missing system libraries or Chromium failing to start.

One thing for you to decide: your local database still holds the Lockheed IR page captured by the disguised browser. It isn't in the zip. I left it alone rather than delete data; say if you want it removed.

## What you run

```bash

cd foci-screen
git remote remove origin
```

Then create an **empty private repo** on GitHub (no README or .gitignore — this one has both) and:

```bash

git push -u origin main
```

`gh` isn't installed here, and the repo is yours to create, so that last step is yours. **Wait for CI to go green before touching Render.**

## CI is the first real Docker build

I still can't build the images locally, so I made the first push do it. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` builds both images, boots the API and checks `/health`, the web UI inside the wheel, and that auth refuses an unauthenticated call.

The check I most wanted is on the worker: **Chromium actually launching as the unprivileged user.** `playwright install --with-deps` runs as root and the image then drops to uid 10001. If the browser ends up unreadable, every investor-relations page silently goes unread in production while the worker looks perfectly healthy.

## Things found along the way

- **Line endings.** Git on Windows checks files out as CRLF, and Docker builds from your working copy. A stray carriage return in a `RUN` step fails as `/bin/sh: 1: \r: not found` — confusing at the exact moment you least want it. A `.gitattributes` now pins everything to LF; verified via `git ls-files --eol`.
- **`/health` now reports the version**, so you can confirm Render is running the build you meant. The install check fails if it's missing or stale.
- **The release zip had been staged** into the first commit. Caught in the pre-commit audit and excluded.
- **I checked `render.yaml` mechanically** — every `fromDatabase`/`fromService` reference resolves, and no service is missing its database URL or contact address.

## Two caveats

**The blueprint has never been applied to a live Render account.** The likeliest drift is `type: redis`, which Render renamed to Key Value; if it's rejected, change that one line to `keyvalue`. It's in DEPLOY.md.

**I amended a commit.** PowerShell wrote the message file with a byte-order mark, leaving an invisible character at the front of the subject line. The commit was unpushed and the file contents were untouched — but flagging it since you'd otherwise see a hash that doesn't match what I reported mid-run.

The working copy still lives in a session workspace that disappears with the session, so clone the bundle somewhere permanent before that happens.

## What I changed, and why

## Getting it onto GitHub

The repo currently holds `foci-screen-all-code.md` and a file named `9-22-26-6:47pm` — the bundle was uploaded through the web UI, which can't work: a bundle is a *clone source*, so uploading it just stores a binary blob.

That colon in the filename is also a real problem — **Windows forbids `:` in filenames**, so cloning that repo on your machine fails with an invalid-path error. My merge drops it.

So I added `tools/push_to_github.ps1`, which I tested end to end:

```powershell
.\tools\push_to_github.ps1 -Bundle .\foci-screen-ready.bundle
```

It verifies the bundle, clones it, points the remote at your repo, confirms the push is **additive rather than a rewrite**, and pushes. Add `-WhatIfOnly` to watch it stop just before pushing. Your existing commits stay as ancestors. The push still needs your interactive GitHub sign-in — that's the one step I can't do.

Writing that script caught a bug of its own: PowerShell treats anything a command writes to stderr as fatal, and `git bundle verify` reports *success* there, so the first version died on a healthy bundle.

## Subcontractor screening

The roadmap's highest-value item. The tool ranked contractors by obligated dollars — which is a ranking of primes, while FOCI risk concentrates in small suppliers. `--subcontractors N` now reaches them. A live Navy run surfaced `INCHCAPE SHIPPING SERVICES(JAPAN) LTD.` under a Navy prime; no prime-level query would have shown it.

Probing the live API first was worth it — three findings that would each have been a silent defect:

- **The sub-agency filter is ignored.** Defense + Navy returns byte-identical results to Defense alone: 42 Navy, 26 Air Force, 19 Army. A "Navy" screen would have reported Army suppliers as Navy. Now filtered client-side — verified live at 0 wrong-agency rows.
- **The dollar figures are fiction.** Largest in the sample was a **$5.0B subaward to a machine shop**. Ordering is by date instead, and notices label the figure as self-reported rather than quoting it as an obligation.
- **Some subawardees are people.** `JOSHUA D GOODWIN` came back under a Navy prime. Screening a named individual, then writing to their customer's contracting officer about them, is a different act from screening a company — so it's skipped by default. The test is a name heuristic that **"Blue Origin" would also trip**, so skipped names are listed in the run notes rather than vanishing.

Also: FPDS returns the *prime's* vendor record for a subaward lookup. Copying it across would have stamped Northrop's ownership and parent onto a small supplier — the exact misattribution this tool exists to avoid. Enrichment now stops after the officer.

I also fixed pre-existing lint errors that would have turned **your first CI run red** before it ever built an image.

215 tests and the CI lint both pass from a fresh clone of the bundle.

### Response · 2026-09-23 16:30 UTC

*Tools used: PowerShell×2, SendUserFile*

This round: **the feedback loop** — the roadmap item everything else was waiting on.

Every weight in the risk engine was set by my judgement. `IP_COLLATERAL` at 7.0, the China multiplier at 3.0, the ×0.35 damping for periodic reports — none of it has ever been checked against an outcome. Now reviewers mark each signal **confirmed / false positive / unclear**, right under the evidence they're already reading, and `#/rules` reports precision per rule, worst first. That's the basis for retiring a rule, tuning a weight, or ranking by risk instead of dollars — none of which were possible without a target variable.

Three counting decisions I want to flag, because each could have gone the flattering way:

- **"Unclear" stays out of the denominator.** A reviewer who couldn't tell hasn't said the rule was wrong; folding that in would punish rules that raise genuinely hard questions.
- **An unjudged rule reads "not measured", never 100%.** Unmeasured is not perfect.
- **Verdicts are revisable** — unlike notice decisions, which are final because they're *actions*. A verdict is a judgement, and someone who looks again and changes their mind is producing better data, not a second data point.

Signals are keyed by a hash of rule + evidence rather than rendered wording, so rewording a rationale doesn't orphan existing verdicts. Findings written by older builds get an id computed on read, so they can still be marked.

Verified end to end in the browser against the live database: recorded a verdict → it appeared on the precision page → controls reset after cleanup.

**Two things worth your attention.** I marked `EDGAR-8K-1.01` as a false positive during that test — a rule I have no grounds to call wrong, since it fired on a real Lockheed 8-K Item 1.01. I removed it; the first real measurements shouldn't start from my guess.

And one full test run took **835 seconds** instead of the usual 20, which looked alarming. I measured it properly rather than assuming: 236 tests in 24 seconds, slowest 0.51s. It was machine contention, not a regression — nothing fixed, nothing to fix.

Still outstanding, unchanged: **nothing is on GitHub yet.** The remote is still at your web-UI commit, and no CI has ever run, so both Docker images remain unbuilt. `.\tools\push_to_github.ps1 -Bundle .\foci-screen-ready.bundle` is the one step I can't do for you.

### Response · 2026-09-23 17:48 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, Write, PowerShell×2, SendUserFile*

This round: **entity resolution**, which the roadmap called the weakest link in the chain.

The CIK mapping is the highest-consequence guess the tool makes. EDGAR full-text search is *constrained* by CIK, so a wrong answer doesn't come back empty — it comes back with another registrant's exhibits attached to this contractor's name. That was the first bug this project ever had. Until now it was recomputed by name similarity on every screen, against a ticker file that changes, with no way to correct one.

Mappings are now stored with their confidence, and **a human verdict outranks the matcher permanently, in both directions**:

- **Confirmed** is never re-resolved — no lookup is even attempted. The matcher doesn't get a second opinion after a person has decided.
- **Rejected** means *no registrant identified*, not merely "unreviewed". Re-deciding by similarity next run would reintroduce exactly the misattribution the reviewer just removed, so attribution stops until someone says otherwise.

Unreviewed mappings still track the matcher, so improving it keeps helping everything nobody has looked at yet.

I also made the resolver return the nearest registrant even when it *rejects* a match. The review queue can then show how close the runner-up was — the difference between "certainly this registrant" and "the closest of several near misses" is exactly what a reviewer needs to see, and reporting only "no match" hides it.

Verified in the browser against live data: the card showed Lockheed at CIK 0000936468, 100% name match, unreviewed; confirming it recorded attribution and removed the confirm action. I left that verdict in place because it's true — Lockheed Martin genuinely is that CIK.

**Two notes.** The fresh-clone test run took 155 seconds this time versus 32 locally; same 254 tests passing, so it's the machine again rather than the code — I'm flagging it rather than reading anything into it.

And the thing that hasn't moved: **nothing is on GitHub.** The remote is still at your web-UI commit, no CI has ever run, and both Docker images remain unbuilt. That one command is still the gate:

```powershell
.\tools\push_to_github.ps1 -Bundle .\foci-screen-ready.bundle
```

### Response · 2026-09-24 16:11 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, Write, PowerShell×2, SendUserFile*

This round closes the loop opened last time: the precision report could show a rule wasn't earning its place, but acting on that meant editing the engine and redeploying. Now each rule has an on/off switch and a weight, per tenant, on the same page as the measurement that justifies changing it.

Three properties where the sloppy version was tempting:

- **A disabled rule is removed before the compound rule runs.** Otherwise a rule you'd retired could still escalate a contractor by pairing with another one — back-door survival.
- **Rescaling recomputes the severity band.** A signal scored down to 2.0 that still read "critical" would be worse than not rescaling at all.
- **Reset deletes the override rather than writing weight 1.0**, so a later change to the engine default still reaches tenants who never disagreed with it.

Settings are read per screen and applied between evaluation and finding-assembly, so the engine stays a pure function of its inputs and never touches a store. The rule catalogue is built from rule ids actually observed in findings — the ids are string literals inside the rule functions, and any hand-maintained list would drift silently.

Verified in the browser end to end: disabled a rule, saw it persist with a "tuned" marker, reset it, confirmed no override left in the database.

Two test assumptions of mine were wrong and worth noting, since both were *my* errors rather than the code's: `band(2.0)` is "info", not "low"; and the compound rule requires the foreign signal to name an actual jurisdiction, so my control case wasn't firing. Fixed both rather than weakening the assertions.

**Still the gate:** nothing is on GitHub. The remote sits at your web-UI commit, no CI has run, and both Docker images remain unbuilt — so the images stay the one part of this I've never been able to verify.

```powershell
.\tools\push_to_github.ps1 -Bundle .\foci-screen-ready.bundle
```

## Prompt · 2026-09-24 16:44 UTC

coudl this tool be purely python?

---

### Response · 2026-09-24 16:44 UTC

Let me quantify what's actually not Python before answering.

---

### Response · 2026-09-24 16:44 UTC

*Tools used: PowerShell*

Mostly, yes — and two of the three non-Python pieces are easy wins.

The non-Python code is **1,297 lines of JavaScript, 480 CSS, 57 HTML**, plus a 109-line PowerShell script. Everything else is config or prose.

**The PowerShell script should just be Python** — it only shells out to `git`, and rewriting it makes it work on macOS and Linux too. That's strictly better; say the word and I'll do it.

**HTML and CSS can't become Python.** A browser needs them, and any Python UI framework just generates them for you.

**The JavaScript is the real question, and most of it exists for one reason:** the API authenticates with a bearer token in `localStorage`, so every single interaction — including downloading a `.eml` — must go through `fetch()` with a header instead of a plain link or form. Switch the browser path to a session cookie and you could server-render HTML with plain `<form>` POSTs and delete nearly all of it. Templates in Jinja, logic in Python, testable in pytest.

What you'd lose is in-place updates: marking a verdict, toggling a rule, or hitting the disclaimer validation would each become a page reload rather than a change under your cursor. The notice editor preserving your text through a rejection still works fine server-side — it just costs a round trip.

I'd avoid Streamlit, NiceGUI or Reflex here. They'd remove *your* JavaScript by shipping someone else's, plus a heavy dependency, and they'd break the property that this app has no build step, no CDN and runs on a network that blocks outside origins.

So: **a Jinja + plain-forms rewrite is genuinely achievable and would leave HTML/CSS as the only non-Python code.** The honest tradeoff is a less responsive UI and a rewrite of something that currently works and is verified. If the goal is "a Python team can maintain every line," it's worth it; if it's aesthetic purity, I'd spend the effort on the USPTO key instead.

---

## Why it happened

`render.yaml` marks `FOCI_API_KEYS` as `sync: false`. That's deliberate — it's a secret, so the blueprint refuses to carry it and Render *prompts* you at apply time. Skip that prompt and the value is empty, which is the fail-closed state: the service boots, answers `/health`, and refuses everything else.

## What I changed

You'd have hit a second wall immediately: pasting the key into Render **without** the `default:` prefix was silently discarded, producing the identical blank page. A bare key is now accepted and treated as the `default` tenant — the tenant the CLI writes as, so it's what a single-tenant deployment wants anyway.

That leniency stops at the prefix. An empty value, `,`, or `acme:` still leave the API closed — being forgiving about formatting shouldn't become forgiving about having no key at all.

One behavioural change worth flagging: an existing test asserted that a bare entry was discarded. That was the bug, so I updated the test to state the new contract rather than working around it. 278 tests pass.

### Response · 2026-09-24 17:16 UTC

*Tools used: PowerShell, Edit*

Saved for future reference. The app itself is healthy — running **0.8.0**, serving the UI and `app.js` correctly. But `/health` exposes three problems, and only one is the blank page:

```json
{"status":"ok","version":"0.8.0","queue":"thread","database":"sqlite","authenticated":false}
```

**1. `authenticated: false`** — `FOCI_API_KEYS` still isn't set, so the page stays blank. The dashboard step hasn't taken effect yet.

**2. `database: sqlite`** — this is the serious one. `DATABASE_URL` isn't set, so it's writing to SQLite on Render's **ephemeral disk**. Every restart and every deploy wipes it. For this tool that's worse than it sounds: the stored snapshots *are* the baseline for change detection, so after each wipe every document reads as new and the next run produces an alert storm about nothing.

**3. `queue: thread`** — `REDIS_URL` isn't set, so screens would run inside the web process and die with it, mid-run, with no record.

Together these say the service was created as a **standalone web service**, not from the blueprint — which fits the name `contractwatcher` rather than `foci-api` from `render.yaml`.

## What I'd do

**Right now**, to see the site work: set `FOCI_API_KEYS` = `default:<redacted-api-key>` on the service and save. It'll restart and the UI will open. The dashboard will show zeros, because that database has never had a screen run against it.

**Before relying on it**, redeploy from the blueprint — **New → Blueprint**, pointed at the repo. That creates Postgres, Redis, the API, the worker and the nightly cron, wired together, instead of one web service holding data it will lose. You can delete `contractwatcher` afterwards.

Adding Postgres and Redis to the existing service by hand works too, but you'd still need a separate worker service, at which point the blueprint is less effort and matches what's already tested in CI.

Worth knowing: `/health` answers all of this without a key, so it's the first thing to check any time the site looks empty.

### Response · 2026-09-24 17:43 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, Bash×2, PowerShell×3, mcp__Claude_Browser__preview_stop, mcp__Claude_Browser__preview_start, mcp__Claude_Browser__browser_batch, mcp__Claude_Browser__javascript_tool*

Understood — here's the manual path, and it's shorter than the blueprint.

**Step 1 is the whole fix for data loss.** One resource, one variable:

1. **New → Postgres**, plan `basic-256mb` (free expires at 30 days, and losing the database is the thing you're fixing), same region as the web service.
2. Copy its **Internal Database URL**.
3. On `contractwatcher`: **Environment → Add Environment Variable**, `DATABASE_URL` = that URL → **Save changes**.

```bash
curl https://contractwatcher-4wxb.onrender.com/health
```

You want `"database":"postgres"` and the SQLite warning gone. Stop there and you have a deployment that keeps its data and screens correctly — `queue: thread` runs screens in the web process, which is exactly how the CLI has always run them.

**Steps 2 and 3 buy robustness, and cost money.** A queue (Key Value + a Background Worker from `./Dockerfile.worker`, plan `standard`) means a screen survives a restart mid-run. Do both or neither: `REDIS_URL` without a worker accepts screens, queues them, runs none, and reports `queue: rq` — healthier-looking than what it replaced.

**Or skip the worker entirely.** Point the CLI at the same Postgres and screen from your own machine; Render just displays the results:

```bash
pip install ".[postgres]"
```

```bash
export DATABASE_URL="<the EXTERNAL connection string from Render>" && foci-screen screen --agency "Department of Defense" --months 6 --entities 5
```

Same code path the worker would run — the only difference is whose minutes get spent. Use the **external** URL from outside Render, keep it out of the repo, and note your network has to allow outbound 5432.

**One thing I hardened while writing this.** If the service was built without the `postgres` extra, setting `DATABASE_URL` gives you a service that boots, serves the UI, and then fails every query with `No module named 'psycopg'` — which tells you nothing. The store now raises a message naming the extra, and `/health` warns before a request goes wrong. If you see that warning after step 1, it's a build-command problem: `Dockerfile` already installs `.[api,queue,postgres]`, so a Docker service is fine; a native Python service needs its build command set to `pip install ".[api,queue,postgres]"`.

286 tests pass, lint clean. You've pushed `6611c59`; `1002931` is waiting in [DEPLOY.md](DEPLOY.md) and the store — push it when convenient, though nothing in step 1 depends on it.

### Response · 2026-09-24 18:02 UTC

*Tools used: Edit, Bash, PowerShell*

Did a full pass. Found four defects, all the same shape — a number that's wrong without looking wrong.

**1. Totals were sums of a page.** `contracts_where` returns the 200 best-funded awards; the entity, officer and agency routes summed that list and published it as "obligated". A contractor with 260 awards had its total understated by whatever the tail held, with nothing on the page saying it was a subtotal. Totals now come from `COUNT`/`SUM` in SQL over every row; the list stays capped and the page says *"showing the 200 largest of 260."*

**2. Sub-agency pages listed no officers at all.** The agency page took the tenant's 200 best-funded officers *globally*, then kept the ones whose `agency` matched — comparing against `MAX(agency)`, which for an officer working across two agencies is whichever sorted higher, and for a sub-agency is never a match. A small agency sitting behind 200 better-funded officers elsewhere also showed none of its own. Both lists are now `GROUP BY` in SQL matching agency or sub-agency.

**3. The one that matters most for what this tool is for.** The contracts upsert updated `entity_name` but not `entity_key`, `recipient_uei`, or `country_of_incorporation`. So a re-screen after a **novation** left the award filed under the old contractor while displaying the new contractor's name — an internally inconsistent row attributing the work to the wrong company. And an entity that re-registered in a covered nation kept reading as it did the day it was first seen, on the single most direct structural FOCI signal in that table. The update list is now every column except the row's identity.

**4. Search wildcards weren't escaped.** `SPACE_SYSTEMS` matched `SPACEXSYSTEMS`, and searching `%` returned everything as though it all matched. Parameterised throughout, so never an injection — just a wrong answer.

**Evidence, not assertion:** I wrote nine tests first, stashed the fixes, and confirmed all nine fail on the previous commit and pass on this one. Then verified against the running site by seeding a contractor with 260 awards — the page read **260 / $294K** with the truncation note, and the sub-agency page listed its officer with the correct count. I removed the seeded rows afterward; your local database is back to its 15 awards.

296 tests pass, lint clean. Committed as `c07f187` and fast-forwarded into your clone — `1002931` went through on your end, so that's the one commit outstanding.

One thing I deliberately did **not** build: storing the truth about a novation is the floor, not the goal. An award moving to a different UEI, or a contractor's incorporation country moving to a covered nation, should raise a *signal* with the old and new values as evidence — the way a changed document does. Contracts have no equivalent of the snapshot machinery. I've written it up as the top roadmap item that needs no API key; say the word and I'll build it next.

**Development: awards that change hands are now events, not overwritten values.**

This was the roadmap item I flagged. The document side has always held that a *change* is stronger evidence than a standing fact; the contract record had no equivalent — it was written over in place, so a novation read afterwards as though it had always been so. Now:

| What moved | Rule | Fires at |
|---|---|---|
| Award sits against a different contractor/UEI | `CHANGE-NOVATION-01` | medium |
| Registered seat moved | `CHANGE-COUNTRY-01` | weighted by destination |
| FPDS foreign-owned newly true | `CHANGE-FOREIGN-OWNED-01` | medium |

Verified end to end: a contractor moving **USA → CHN** and self-certifying foreign-owned produced `CHANGE-COUNTRY-01` at **high (19.2)** and `CHANGE-FOREIGN-OWNED-01` at **medium (12.8)**, and both rows rendered on the contractor page under "Changes to the award record." A move to Canada scores materially lower — the jurisdiction carries the weight, not the fact of moving.

Three things deliberately *don't* fire: a first sighting (a baseline is not a transition), a withdrawn certification (as likely cleanup as news), and an officer reassignment (changes who to notify, not whether to).

**Writing those tests caught a fault in yesterday's commit.** Widening the upsert meant a blank incoming value now overwrote a good stored one — so a failed enrichment could *erase* a country of incorporation, the exact field this feature exists to watch. Blank values and zero amounts now leave the column alone.

- **`/health` now carries `ephemeral_storage`** as an explicit flag, rather than leaving the UI to grep warning prose that would break the next time the wording improved.

**The shortest fix, which I'd missed until now: a persistent disk.** No new service, no blueprint, two settings:

1. Service → **Settings** → **Disks** → **Add Disk**, mount path `/var/data`, size 1 GB
2. **Environment** → `FOCI_DB` = `/var/data/foci_screen.db`
3. **Save changes**

Then `/health` should read `"ephemeral_storage": false`.

**One caveat that decides which route you take:** Render requires a paid *instance type* for a disk. If `contractwatcher` is on the free instance type, disks aren't available — and free instances also spin down when idle, which is its own wipe, and is very likely what happened to you overnight. In that case it's Postgres (`DATABASE_URL`), which works on any plan. Both are written up in [DEPLOY.md](DEPLOY.md) §1.

I have **not** re-run the screen. It would work, look right for a few hours, and vanish the same way — I'd rather you fix the storage first, then I'll re-run it and confirm the site holds the data across a restart.

### Response · 2026-09-25 15:14 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×4, PowerShell×2, Bash*

That settles it: **Postgres is your only persistence option**, and free Postgres works fine for this.

But the free plan has a second trap I hadn't handled, so I fixed it before telling you to do anything. Free instances spin down after ~15 minutes without a request — and a screen running in the web process dies with it. Nothing was left alive to write the closing row, so the run sat at `running` **forever**, and a client would poll a screen that ended hours earlier. The API now sweeps those on startup: they read `interrupted`, with the reason. Only in thread mode — with a worker, a run marked running may be genuinely in progress, and the minutes a screen spends waiting on government APIs are exactly when a wrong sweep would land.

That sweep is the first thing in the service to touch the database, so I wrapped it: a bad `DATABASE_URL` must not take down `/health` and the banner, which are where you'd read what went wrong. There's a test for that specifically.

**What to do, in order:**

1. **New → Postgres**, free tier, same region. Diarise the 30-day expiry — Render deletes it, and the snapshot baseline goes with it.
2. Copy the **Internal Database URL** → service **Environment** → `DATABASE_URL` → **Save changes**.
3. Confirm: `/health` should read `"database":"postgres"` and `"ephemeral_storage": false`.

**Then screen from your own machine, not from the site.** On the free plan this is the recommended route, not a workaround — your laptop doesn't spin down mid-run:

```bash
pip install ".[postgres]"
```

```bash
export DATABASE_URL="<the EXTERNAL connection string from Render>" && foci-screen screen --agency "Department of Defense" --sub-agency "Department of the Navy" --months 6 --entities 5
```

Render then just displays the results. Needs outbound 5432 open on your network, and keep that URL out of the repo — it carries a password.

If you'd rather screen from the site itself, keep `max_entities` to 3–5 so it finishes inside the idle window.

312 tests pass (1 skipped — no Redis here to exercise the queue guard), lint clean. Committed as `92c3828`, fast-forwarded into your clone; `06ec454` went through on your end.

Once `DATABASE_URL` is set, tell me and I'll re-run the screen and verify the data survives a restart — which is the thing we haven't yet been able to prove.

```
FOCI-PORTFOLIO-1.eNpljk1rhDAURf9KyKKbOiV-zphd1GeMMYkkURllVoVCoVQoQzel_70Zt10-zrn33R_8ium2Y…c33f6f44
```

That design choice is downstream of everything we just went through. A saved dashboard held server-side is a saved dashboard that disappears when the database does — which has already happened to you once. A key in a file on your machine opens the same dashboard in another browser, on another machine, against a different deployment, or against a database rebuilt from nothing. **How you use it:** the **Portfolio** tab builds a key from a typed list of companies, or from everything screened so far, then **Save to a file** writes a `.txt` with a header explaining what it is. Paste it back any time — line breaks from a text editor are fine.

Two deliberate properties:

- **A truncated key is refused, not partly loaded.** I cut 60 characters out of a real key and got back *"That key is damaged and cannot be read"* — a 400 with the reason. A portfolio that quietly loads eight of its ten companies leaves two companies unwatched by someone who believes they're watching them.
- **A key is not a credential.** It holds company names and nothing else; you still need the API key to reach the deployment. Anyone you send it to can open the same list — which makes it a reasonable way to hand a watchlist to a colleague.

A company in the key that a deployment has never screened shows as *not screened here* rather than being dropped — that's your "what to screen next" list.

### Response · 2026-09-25 15:44 UTC

*Tools used: Edit, PowerShell×2, Bash*

The Portfolio tab **is** on your live site — your browser is running pre-deploy code.

**Right now, to use it:** hard-refresh the page — **Ctrl+Shift+R** (or Ctrl+F5). The tab will appear.

**The diagnosis.** The live service answers `/v1/portfolio` with a 400 (a real response from the new code, not a 404), and the `app.js` on the server contains the feature. What your browser holds is the `index.html` and `app.js` from *before* the deploy — no Portfolio tab, no box to paste into. I made the same mistake one tool call earlier in this session: my first check read a cached `/app.js` and I nearly told you the deploy hadn't landed. Fetching with the cache bypassed showed the feature present all along. That's twice this has caught me, which is a good sign it's a design fault rather than bad luck.

**The fix.** `index.html` was served straight from `StaticFiles`, so a browser could keep it indefinitely. It's now served by a route that sets `Cache-Control: no-cache, must-revalidate` and rewrites the asset URLs with a content fingerprint:

```
./app.js?v=b3b07d4105f9
./styles.css?v=b3b07d4105f9
```

Any deploy that changes those files changes the hash, so the cached copies stop being used. I used a content hash rather than the version number deliberately — two deploys usually share a version, and the failure is silent: a page that looks current while missing what just shipped.

**I also tested the path you reported, not the one I'd tested before.** Previously I'd only clicked "Use everything screened here." This time I typed a 352-character key into the box by hand and clicked Open — the six-company dashboard loaded correctly. So the input itself was never broken.

333 tests pass, lint clean. Committed as `c081446`, fast-forwarded into your clone; `4e31774` is already live, so **this one needs pushing** — and it's the last time a deploy should be invisible to a browser.

One note: after you push `c081446`, you may need *one* more hard refresh to pick up the new `index.html`. Every deploy after that is automatic.

### Response · 2026-09-25 16:00 UTC

*Tools used: Edit×2, Bash, PowerShell, mcp__Claude_Browser__javascript_tool*

Your `c081446` push landed — the live site now serves `no-cache` with fingerprinted assets, and the Portfolio tab is in the HTML. That trap is closed permanently.

**On the Redis warning: the advice it gave you was impossible to follow.** Free Render has no background worker to add, so the banner carried a permanent instruction you couldn't act on — and a notice nobody can act on gets read as noise, including the parts that matter. It was also warning about the wrong thing.

**The genuinely dangerous state is a queue with nothing listening.** Redis set, no worker running: screens are accepted, they queue, and nothing ever runs them — while `/health` reports `queue: rq` and looks *healthier* than what it replaced. The API now counts workers on the queue and warns only when there are none. A count it can't obtain isn't reported as zero, so Redis being slow to answer a diagnostic won't raise a false alarm.

**Behind the warning was a real gap, which I closed.** Findings were written only after *every* contractor had been screened. A screen dying at the third contractor lost the first two as well — their awards were already indexed, so the run threw away precisely the part that had taken the work. Each finding is now saved as its contractor finishes. A screen that dies keeps everything completed, and the run reads `interrupted`. Suppression is unaffected — it reads other runs, never the current one.

**To clear the banner, two things:**

1. Push `8931005` (it's the only commit outstanding now).
2. On Render → your service → **Environment**, add `FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS` = `true` → **Save changes**.

That flag changes nothing about how screens run. It records that running them in the web process is a decision rather than an oversight. It silences that one line only — a closed API or a disposable database would still say so. I've deliberately left it to you rather than defaulting it off, because on a deployment where a worker *is* available, that reminder should keep nagging.

Given no worker is available to you, the practical guidance is now in DEPLOY.md: keep `max_entities` to 3–5 from the site (the two screens I ran finished in about 2½ minutes), or run bigger ones from the CLI against the Render Postgres, where nothing spins down mid-run.

342 tests pass, lint clean.

## Prompt · 2026-09-25 16:03 UTC

where do i add "FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS = true"

---

### Response · 2026-09-25 16:03 UTC

Same place you added `DATABASE_URL` — it's a service environment variable, not a file.

1. **dashboard.render.com** → click your **contractwatcher** service
2. Left sidebar → **Environment**
3. **Add Environment Variable**
4. **Key:** `FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS`  **Value:** `true`
5. **Save changes** — the service restarts on its own

Key and value are two separate fields. Don't paste `FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS = true` into one box — the name would come out with a trailing space and it'd be silently ignored.

**One sequencing point that will otherwise look like a failure:** the code that reads this flag is in `8931005`, which isn't pushed yet. If you set the variable first, the banner will still be there after the restart — not because the variable is wrong, but because the running build doesn't know the flag exists. Push `8931005` first, let Render deploy it, *then* add the variable. Or do it in either order and just expect the banner to clear only after both have landed.

To confirm it worked:

```bash
curl https://contractwatcher-4wxb.onrender.com/health
```

`warnings` should come back as `[]`.

If you also run screens locally, the same flag goes in your `.env` file at `<home>\code\foci-screen\.env` as a line reading `FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS=true` — though locally the warning never fires anyway, since threads are the right answer on a laptop.

### Response · 2026-09-25 20:36 UTC

*Tools used: Bash, PowerShell*

Both complaints had one root cause, and it was a real design gap — not a UI bug.

**You couldn't search for new firms because there was no way to *get* a new firm in.** `agency` was a required field on every screen, so the only route into the database was to screen an entire department and take whoever turned up. Search reads the screened population, so a firm nobody had screened matched nothing — correctly, with nothing to do about it.

**And the "default portfolio" is my fault.** Those companies — Lockheed, Electric Boat, Boeing — are whoever the Navy screen *I* ran happened to surface. That's not a portfolio you chose; it's the residue of my verification.

**What's fixed:**

- **Screen a company by name.** `--recipient "RAYTHEON COMPANY"`, or `{"recipient": "..."}` over the API. Naming both a recipient and an agency narrows to one contractor's work for one department. USAspending matches against name *and* UEI, so a pasted UEI works too. I verified this against the live API rather than just the request shape — Raytheon and Huntington Ingalls each return their own awards.
- **The fruitless search is now where screening starts.** Search "Raytheon", get told this database hasn't looked at it, click **Screen "Raytheon"**.
- **A run page to watch it on.** Screens take minutes; until now progress was only visible by polling the API by hand. It refreshes itself and shows the live step.
- **Remove a screen you didn't want.** The overview now lists the runs that produced what's on it, so there's a route from an unwanted contractor back to the screen that brought it in. Removal asks twice and prints the counts first — those counts are the only way to tell a stray screen from the run holding a quarter's work. Shared document snapshots are never touched.

**Verified end to end in the browser:** searched "Raytheon" against a database that had never seen it → offered a screen → ran to completion (25 awards, 3 contractors, 3 findings) → became searchable. Then the two-step removal reported *"1 notices, 18 contracts, 3 findings"* and left the unrelated 15 awards alone. Your local database is back exactly where it started.

358 tests pass, lint clean. Committed as `38375a6`.

**You now have two commits to push** — `8931005` (the queue warning) and `38375a6`. Once they deploy, open the run for `0958bee43927` and remove it if you want those three primes off your dashboard — then screen the firms you actually care about by name, and build a portfolio key from them.

### Response · 2026-09-25 21:16 UTC

All three are in, verified with real keystrokes rather than synthetic events.

**The overview opens empty now.** It used to show everything in the database — whoever the last departmental screen happened to surface — which is exactly the "default portfolio" you objected to. It now shows the companies you picked, and until you pick some it explains what a portfolio is and gets out of the way, with the run history underneath for reference.

The first add starts a portfolio, so there's nothing to set up first.

**The search box suggests as you type**, from two sources kept visually distinct: what's been screened here (instant, straight from the database), and contractors that exist in federal contracting but have **never been screened here** — labelled as such, and choosing one offers a screen.

**One thing I want to be explicit about, because your wording could be read either way:** typing never *screens*. A screen takes minutes and walks half a dozen government APIs; firing one per keystroke would be both absurd and a rude way to treat a public service. Typing suggests; screening stays behind a button. There's a test holding that line — six prefixes typed, zero jobs enqueued.

**And a measurement that shaped the design.** I tested USAspending's autocomplete before building on it, and it's the opposite of what a type-ahead wants: `huntington ing` answered in ~1s, but `rayth` and `lockhe` both **timed out at 20s** — the cost is in how many recipients the prefix matches, so the short prefixes you type first are the expensive ones. So the remote half is debounced, floored at four characters, capped at a 4-second timeout, cached per prefix, and allowed to return nothing. Local suggestions never wait on it. (On a later run `rayth` came back in 1.2s — that variability is what the defensive design absorbs, not a sign the first measurement was wrong.)

**Verified end to end:** typed `lockh` → dropdown showed *LOCKHEED MARTIN CORPORATION · contractor · 4 award(s)* → chose it → **Add to portfolio** → "Portfolio now holds 1 company" → the overview then showed that portfolio, **$102.11B across 4 awards**, instead of the six contractors it used to open on.

372 tests pass, lint clean. Committed as `177b79f` — that's now three waiting to push (`8931005`, `38375a6`, `177b79f`).

# Package configuration


## `pyproject.toml`

<a id="pyprojecttoml"></a>

```toml
[build-system]
requires = ["setuptools>=68"]
build-backend = "setuptools.build_meta"

[project]
name = "foci-screen"
version = "0.8.0"
description = "Screen federal contracts and contractor disclosures for FOCI and intellectual-property risk changes"
readme = "README.md"
requires-python = ">=3.10"
license = { text = "Proprietary" }
dependencies = [
    "requests>=2.31",
    "beautifulsoup4>=4.12",
]

[project.optional-dependencies]
# TLS-inspecting corporate proxies: use the OS trust store instead of certifi.
proxy = ["truststore>=0.9"]
# Step 3 delivery. Not installed by default — the Gmail path is inert without it.
gmail = [
    "google-api-python-client>=2.100",
    "google-auth-oauthlib>=1.1",
]
api = [
    "fastapi>=0.110",
    "uvicorn[standard]>=0.27",
    "pydantic>=2.6",
]
queue = ["rq>=1.16", "redis>=5.0"]
postgres = ["psycopg[binary]>=3.1"]
# Reads JavaScript-gated investor-relations pages. Needs `playwright install
# chromium` after pip; the package alone ships no browser.
browser = ["playwright>=1.44"]
# Everything a deployed instance needs.
server = [
    "fastapi>=0.110",
    "uvicorn[standard]>=0.27",
    "pydantic>=2.6",
    "rq>=1.16",
    "redis>=5.0",
    "psycopg[binary]>=3.1",
    "playwright>=1.44",
]
dev = ["pytest>=8.0", "ruff>=0.4", "httpx>=0.27"]

[project.scripts]
foci-screen = "foci_screen.cli:main"
foci-worker = "foci_screen.worker:main"
foci-scheduler = "foci_screen.scheduler:main"

[tool.setuptools.packages.find]
include = ["foci_screen*"]

[tool.setuptools.package-data]
foci_screen = ["web/*"]

[tool.ruff]
line-length = 96
target-version = "py310"

[tool.ruff.lint]
select = ["E", "F", "W", "I", "UP", "B"]
ignore = ["B008"]

[tool.ruff.lint.per-file-ignores]
# Jurisdiction and terminology tables. Each entry is one row of data — a name,
# a tier, a multiplier and its patterns — and wrapping them to 96 columns makes
# the table harder to scan and to diff than leaving the rows intact.
"foci_screen/risk/lexicon.py" = ["E501"]
```

# Core


## `foci_screen/__init__.py`

<a id="fociscreeninitpy"></a>

```python
"""foci-screen — contract and contractor risk screening.

Screens federal contract awards and the awardee's public disclosures for
changes indicating Foreign Ownership, Control or Influence (FOCI),
intellectual-property collateralisation, or other IP risk, and drafts a notice
to the responsible contracting officer.
"""
__version__ = "0.1.0"

from .config import Config, get_config
from .models import Contract, Entity, Finding, Signal
from .pipeline import Screener, ScreenOptions
from .store import Store

__all__ = ["Config", "get_config", "Contract", "Entity", "Finding", "Signal",
           "ScreenOptions", "Screener", "Store", "__version__"]
```


## `foci_screen/config.py`

<a id="fociscreenconfigpy"></a>

```python
"""Runtime configuration.

Every credential is optional. The tool degrades gracefully: connectors that
need a key report themselves as unavailable rather than failing the run, so a
keyless install still produces findings from USAspending, FPDS, SEC EDGAR,
IAPD and OFAC.
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import os
from dataclasses import dataclass, field
from pathlib import Path

_TRUE = {"1", "true", "yes", "on"}


def _env(name: str, default: str = "") -> str:
    return os.environ.get(name, default).strip()


def _flag(name: str, default: bool = False) -> bool:
    raw = _env(name)
    return raw.lower() in _TRUE if raw else default


def load_dotenv(path: str | Path = ".env") -> None:
    """Minimal .env loader so we don't take a dependency on python-dotenv."""
    p = Path(path)
    if not p.is_file():
        return
    for line in p.read_text(encoding="utf-8").splitlines():
        line = line.strip()
        if not line or line.startswith("#") or "=" not in line:
            continue
        key, _, value = line.partition("=")
        os.environ.setdefault(key.strip(), value.strip().strip('"').strip("'"))


@dataclass
class Config:
    # --- identification (SEC and other .gov APIs require a real contact) ---
    user_agent: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env(
        "FOCI_USER_AGENT", "foci-screen/0.1 (contact-not-set@example.com)"))

    # --- optional API keys ---
    sam_api_key: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("SAM_API_KEY"))
    uspto_api_key: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("USPTO_API_KEY"))
    trade_gov_key: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("TRADE_GOV_API_KEY"))

    # --- storage ---
    db_path: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_DB", "foci_screen.db"))
    cache_dir: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_CACHE", ".cache"))
    out_dir: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_OUT", "out"))
    # Postgres for any deployment with more than one process or an ephemeral
    # filesystem. Empty means SQLite at `db_path`. Render injects DATABASE_URL.
    database_url: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("DATABASE_URL"))

    # --- job queue ---
    # Empty means run screens inline in a background thread — correct for the
    # CLI and local development, not for a deployment where a screen must
    # survive a web process restart.
    redis_url: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("REDIS_URL"))
    job_timeout: int = field(default_factory=lambda: int(_env("FOCI_JOB_TIMEOUT", "3600")))
    # Set where a queue is not available — a single free-tier service, say —
    # to record that running screens in the web process is a decision rather
    # than an oversight. It changes nothing about how they run; it stops the
    # banner reporting a known trade-off as a fault. The risk does not go
    # away, so keep screens small enough to finish.
    inprocess_screens_ok: bool = field(
        default_factory=lambda: _flag("FOCI_INPROCESS_SCREENS", False))

    # --- headless browser (investor-relations pages) ---
    browser_enabled: bool = field(default_factory=lambda: _flag("FOCI_BROWSER", True))
    browser_timeout: int = field(
        default_factory=lambda: int(_env("FOCI_BROWSER_TIMEOUT", "25")))

    # --- API ---
    api_keys: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_API_KEYS"))
    cors_origins: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_CORS_ORIGINS"))

    # --- HTTP behaviour ---
    timeout: int = field(default_factory=lambda: int(_env("FOCI_TIMEOUT", "45")))
    rate_limit_qps: float = field(
        default_factory=lambda: float(_env("FOCI_QPS", "3.0")))
    cache_ttl_seconds: int = field(
        default_factory=lambda: int(_env("FOCI_CACHE_TTL", "21600")))
    max_retries: int = field(default_factory=lambda: int(_env("FOCI_RETRIES", "3")))

    # --- email / gmail (step 3: wired but inert by default) ---
    gmail_enabled: bool = field(default_factory=lambda: _flag("GMAIL_ENABLED", False))
    gmail_send: bool = field(default_factory=lambda: _flag("GMAIL_SEND", False))
    gmail_credentials: str = field(
        default_factory=lambda: _env("GMAIL_CREDENTIALS", "credentials.json"))
    gmail_token: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("GMAIL_TOKEN", "token.json"))
    gmail_sender: str = field(default_factory=lambda: _env("GMAIL_SENDER"))
    # Safety valve: while set, every draft is addressed here instead of the KO.
    email_redirect_to: str = field(
        default_factory=lambda: _env("FOCI_EMAIL_REDIRECT_TO"))

    def ensure_dirs(self) -> None:
        for d in (self.cache_dir, self.out_dir):
            Path(d).mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    @property
    def managed_host(self) -> str:
        """The platform this is running on, if it is a managed one.

        Used only to decide whether a local-development default has been
        carried into somewhere it will hurt: SQLite on a platform with an
        ephemeral filesystem loses every snapshot on each deploy, and the
        snapshots *are* the baseline the change detection depends on.
        """
        for var, name in (("RENDER", "Render"), ("DYNO", "Heroku"),
                          ("FLY_APP_NAME", "Fly.io"), ("K_SERVICE", "Cloud Run"),
                          ("WEBSITE_INSTANCE_ID", "Azure App Service")):
            if os.environ.get(var):
                return name
        return ""

    @property
    def dsn(self) -> str:
        """Where the store should read and write."""
        return self.database_url or self.db_path

    @property
    def api_key_list(self) -> list[str]:
        return [k.strip() for k in self.api_keys.split(",") if k.strip()]

    @property
    def cors_origin_list(self) -> list[str]:
        return [o.strip() for o in self.cors_origins.split(",") if o.strip()]

    def availability(self) -> dict[str, bool]:
        """Which connectors can actually run with the current credentials."""
        return {
            "usaspending": True,
            "fpds": True,
            "sec_edgar": True,
            "iapd": True,
            "ofac": True,
            "webwatch": True,
            "samgov": bool(self.sam_api_key),
            "uspto": bool(self.uspto_api_key),
            "trade_gov_csl": bool(self.trade_gov_key),
            "headless_browser": self.browser_enabled and _playwright_installed(),
        }


def _playwright_installed() -> bool:
    try:
        import playwright.sync_api  # noqa: F401
    except ImportError:
        return False
    return True


def get_config() -> Config:
    load_dotenv()
    cfg = Config()
    cfg.ensure_dirs()
    return cfg
```


## `foci_screen/models.py`

<a id="fociscreenmodelspy"></a>

```python
"""Core domain objects.

Deliberately plain dataclasses: they serialise to JSON for an API layer later
without dragging a web framework or ORM into the screening core.
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import hashlib
from dataclasses import asdict, dataclass, field
from datetime import date, datetime, timezone
from typing import Any

SEVERITY_ORDER = ["info", "low", "medium", "high", "critical"]


def signal_key(rule_id: str, evidence: str) -> str:
    """Stable identity for one rule firing on one piece of text.

    The rule alone is too coarse — a rule fires many times — and the finding is
    too broad, since a reviewer's verdict is usually about one specific claim
    inside it. Keyed on the evidence rather than the rendered rationale so
    wording changes to a rule do not orphan the verdicts already recorded
    against it.
    """
    raw = f"{rule_id}|{' '.join((evidence or '').split())[:200]}"
    return hashlib.sha256(raw.encode("utf-8", "replace")).hexdigest()[:16]


def _now() -> str:
    return datetime.now(timezone.utc).replace(microsecond=0, tzinfo=None).isoformat() + "Z"


def _clean(obj: Any) -> Any:
    if isinstance(obj, (datetime, date)):
        return obj.isoformat()
    if isinstance(obj, dict):
        return {k: _clean(v) for k, v in obj.items()}
    if isinstance(obj, (list, tuple)):
        return [_clean(v) for v in obj]
    return obj


class Serialisable:
    def to_dict(self) -> dict:
        return _clean(asdict(self))


@dataclass
class ContractingOfficer(Serialisable):
    """Whoever we can actually address the notice to."""
    name: str = ""
    email: str = ""
    phone: str = ""
    office_code: str = ""
    office_name: str = ""
    # How we got here — shown in the email footer so a KO can sanity-check it.
    source: str = ""
    confidence: str = "low"  # low | medium | high

    @property
    def is_addressable(self) -> bool:
        return bool(self.email and "@" in self.email)


@dataclass
class Contract(Serialisable):
    award_id: str
    piid: str = ""
    generated_internal_id: str = ""
    recipient_name: str = ""
    recipient_uei: str = ""
    parent_recipient_name: str = ""
    parent_recipient_uei: str = ""
    awarding_agency: str = ""
    awarding_sub_agency: str = ""
    award_amount: float = 0.0
    start_date: str = ""
    end_date: str = ""
    description: str = ""
    naics_code: str = ""
    naics_description: str = ""
    psc_code: str = ""
    psc_description: str = ""
    solicitation_id: str = ""
    contracting_office_id: str = ""
    recipient_country: str = ""
    country_of_incorporation: str = ""
    state_of_incorporation: str = ""
    foreign_owned_and_located: bool = False
    foreign_funding: str = ""
    officer: ContractingOfficer = field(default_factory=ContractingOfficer)
    # FAR/DFARS data-rights clauses inferred from the requirement description.
    ip_clause_hits: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    source_url: str = ""

    # --- subawards ---------------------------------------------------------
    # A subcontract under someone else's prime. Worth screening because FOCI
    # risk concentrates in small, cash-hungry suppliers, and a ranking by
    # obligated dollars buries exactly those.
    is_subaward: bool = False
    prime_award_id: str = ""
    prime_recipient_name: str = ""
    prime_generated_internal_id: str = ""
    # Subaward values are self-reported by the prime through FSRS and are
    # frequently wrong — observed live: a $4.2B subaward to a small machine
    # shop for "NON-COMPLEX MACHINED." Never rank or threshold on this.
    amount_is_self_reported: bool = False

    @property
    def fpds_piid(self) -> str:
        """The PIID to ask FPDS about.

        For a subaward that is the *prime* contract: the subcontract has no
        contracting officer of its own, and the official who would need to act
        is the one on the prime.
        """
        return self.prime_award_id if self.is_subaward else self.piid

    @property
    def usaspending_url(self) -> str:
        if self.generated_internal_id:
            return f"https://www.usaspending.gov/award/{self.generated_internal_id}"
        return ""

    @property
    def ip_sensitive(self) -> bool:
        return bool(self.ip_clause_hits)


@dataclass
class Entity(Serialisable):
    """A contractor, resolved across the various registries."""
    name: str
    uei: str = ""
    cage: str = ""
    parent_name: str = ""
    parent_uei: str = ""
    cik: str = ""                      # SEC
    tickers: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    domains: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    iapd_crd: str = ""
    countries: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    contracts: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)
    aliases: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)

    def key(self) -> str:
        return (self.uei or self.name).upper().strip()


@dataclass
class Document(Serialisable):
    """A retrieved artefact we can run rules over.

    `text` is normalised plain text; `key` is stable across runs so the store
    can diff this run's copy against the last one.
    """
    source: str            # usaspending | fpds | sec_edgar | iapd | uspto | web | ofac
    key: str
    title: str = ""
    url: str = ""
    text: str = ""
    published: str = ""
    doc_type: str = ""
    meta: dict = field(default_factory=dict)
    fetched_at: str = field(default_factory=_now)

    def sha256(self) -> str:
        return hashlib.sha256(self.text.encode("utf-8", "replace")).hexdigest()


@dataclass
class Change(Serialisable):
    """What is new about a Document since we last looked at it."""
    document_key: str
    source: str
    url: str = ""
    kind: str = "modified"          # new | modified | unchanged
    added_text: str = ""
    previous_sha: str = ""
    current_sha: str = ""
    previous_seen_at: str = ""
    observed_at: str = field(default_factory=_now)

    @property
    def is_material(self) -> bool:
        return self.kind in ("new", "modified") and bool(self.added_text.strip())


@dataclass
class Signal(Serialisable):
    """One rule firing against one piece of evidence."""
    rule_id: str
    category: str          # FOCI | IP_COLLATERAL | IP_TRANSFER | SANCTIONS | STRUCTURE
    severity: str
    score: float
    title: str
    rationale: str
    evidence: str
    source: str
    source_url: str = ""
    jurisdiction: str = ""
    observed_at: str = field(default_factory=_now)
    is_new: bool = False   # surfaced by a change diff rather than a baseline scan
    # Which stored document produced this, so a reviewer can open the diff that
    # contains the matched text rather than pairing the two up by eye.
    document_key: str = ""

    def severity_rank(self) -> int:
        return SEVERITY_ORDER.index(self.severity) if self.severity in SEVERITY_ORDER else 0

    def key(self) -> str:
        return signal_key(self.rule_id, self.evidence)

    def to_dict(self) -> dict:
        # Carried in the payload so a reviewer's verdict can be attached to this
        # exact signal rather than to the finding as a whole.
        return {**super().to_dict(), "signal_id": self.key()}


@dataclass
class Finding(Serialisable):
    """Everything we concluded about one entity in one run."""
    entity: Entity
    signals: list[Signal] = field(default_factory=list)
    contracts: list[Contract] = field(default_factory=list)
    total_score: float = 0.0
    severity: str = "info"
    run_id: str = ""
    generated_at: str = field(default_factory=_now)

    @property
    def obligated_total(self) -> float:
        return sum(c.award_amount for c in self.contracts)

    @property
    def new_signals(self) -> list[Signal]:
        return [s for s in self.signals if s.is_new]

    def by_category(self) -> dict[str, list[Signal]]:
        out: dict[str, list[Signal]] = {}
        for s in self.signals:
            out.setdefault(s.category, []).append(s)
        for v in out.values():
            v.sort(key=lambda s: (-s.severity_rank(), -s.score))
        return out

    def top_officer(self) -> ContractingOfficer:
        """Address the notice to the KO on the largest addressable award."""
        addressable = [c for c in self.contracts if c.officer.is_addressable]
        if not addressable:
            return ContractingOfficer()
        ranked = sorted(
            addressable,
            key=lambda c: ({"high": 2, "medium": 1, "low": 0}.get(c.officer.confidence, 0),
                           c.award_amount),
            reverse=True)
        return ranked[0].officer
```


## `foci_screen/httpclient.py`

<a id="fociscreenhttpclientpy"></a>

```python
"""Polite, cached HTTP client.

Government endpoints throttle aggressively and several of them (SEC in
particular) will ban a User-Agent that doesn't carry a contact address. This
wraps requests with rate limiting, retry/backoff, and an on-disk response
cache so re-running a screen doesn't re-hammer the same URLs.
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import hashlib
import json
import logging
import threading
import time
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any

import requests

try:  # corporate TLS-inspecting proxies need the OS trust store
    import truststore

    truststore.inject_into_ssl()
except Exception:  # pragma: no cover - optional
    pass

log = logging.getLogger("foci.http")


class RateLimiter:
    def __init__(self, qps: float) -> None:
        self._min_interval = 1.0 / qps if qps > 0 else 0.0
        self._last = 0.0
        self._lock = threading.Lock()

    def wait(self) -> None:
        with self._lock:
            delta = time.monotonic() - self._last
            if delta < self._min_interval:
                time.sleep(self._min_interval - delta)
            self._last = time.monotonic()


class HttpClient:
    def __init__(self, config) -> None:
        self.cfg = config
        self.session = requests.Session()
        self.session.headers.update({
            "User-Agent": config.user_agent,
            "Accept-Encoding": "gzip, deflate",
        })
        self.limiter = RateLimiter(config.rate_limit_qps)
        self.cache_dir = Path(config.cache_dir)
        self.cache_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        self.stats = {"hits": 0, "misses": 0, "errors": 0}

    # ------------------------------------------------------------------ cache
    def _cache_path(self, method: str, url: str, body: Any) -> Path:
        raw = f"{method}|{url}|{json.dumps(body, sort_keys=True) if body else ''}"
        digest = hashlib.sha256(raw.encode()).hexdigest()[:32]
        return self.cache_dir / f"{digest}.json"

    def _read_cache(self, path: Path) -> dict | None:
        if not path.is_file():
            return None
        age = time.time() - path.stat().st_mtime
        if age > self.cfg.cache_ttl_seconds:
            return None
        try:
            return json.loads(path.read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
        except Exception:
            return None

    # ----------------------------------------------------------------- request
    def request(self, method: str, url: str, *, json_body: Any = None,
                params: dict | None = None, headers: dict | None = None,
                use_cache: bool = True, timeout: int | None = None,
                max_retries: int | None = None) -> dict:
        """Return {'status', 'text', 'json', 'url'}; never raises on HTTP status.

        `timeout` and `max_retries` override the configured defaults per call.
        Government APIs are worth waiting and retrying for; arbitrary corporate
        websites are not — many stall or drop non-browser agents outright, and
        a full retry budget on each of a dozen candidate URLs turns one slow
        host into a run that never finishes.
        """
        cache_key_body = {"json": json_body, "params": params}
```
